XPO Transport & logistics · US C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering

XPO's Q2 2026 release (30 Jul 2026) states "we continued to improve labor productivity above target by implementing new AI capabilities across the network, enhancing efficiency"; on the same day's call CEO Mario Harik credited "our workforce planning technology" with improving "productivity by nearly 2.5 points versus last year, which outperformed our quarterly target of 1.5%", and said AI trailer-loading pilots delivered "load quality improved by more than 40%, while damages were reduced by 50%".

Mechanism
Two distinct systems. (1) Workforce planning: forecasting freight volume by door and shift to schedule dock and P&D labour against it, which is where the 2.5-point productivity number comes from. (2) Trailer loading: cameras photograph freight at each tier inside the trailer, a vision model scores load quality and instructs the dock worker to correct it in real time - fewer damages and more cube per trailer, i.e. fewer linehaul miles for the same freight. Rollout across the full network is planned for H2 2026.
Why this shape
C4 - non-tech sector, non-engineering function (dock and P&D operations, the bulk of an LTL carrier's cost base). The reason this is a cost entry and not a revenue shape: every number is an input-cost or quality metric and no revenue, yield or price is attributed anywhere. ATTRIBUTION DRIFT worth recording, and the main reason confidence is not higher: the press release attributes above-target labour productivity to "new AI capabilities", while the transcript attributes the specific 2.5-point figure to "workforce planning technology" without saying AI. The same quarter, the same company, two primary documents, two different attributions - METHOD trap 4 (many parents) in miniature. Trap 5 also applies: this is productivity above target, not a cost line that fell; XPO's adjusted LTL operating ratio of 79.9% has network investment, pricing and fleet as co-drivers. The 40%/50% trailer-loading figures are PILOT-SITE results, not network results, and should not be read as realized.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
XPO Q2 2026 earnings call and results release, 2026-07-30
Falsifier
If H2 2026 completes the network-wide trailer-loading rollout and the damage claims ratio and LTL operating ratio do not improve beyond the 0.2% and 79.9% posted this quarter, the pilot numbers did not survive scale. Second falsifier: if the Q3 or Q4 release drops the phrase attributing labour productivity to AI while the productivity target itself stays, the AI framing was decoration on a workforce-scheduling result.
Confidence
medium — Tier A and specific, but the AI attribution and the quantified productivity figure sit in different documents and do not cleanly overlap.
Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis - 'AI and Productivity: What Firms Are Saying on Earnings Calls' (~490,000 transcripts, 5,198 US listed firms, 2000-2025) Cross-sector (research) · US C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering⚠ Counter-evidence

St. Louis Fed research published 31 Jul 2026 (Ozkan, Kalyani, Sullivan) classifying productivity-related sentences in ~490,000 earnings call transcripts from 5,198 US listed firms finds that "approximately 95% of AI-related productivity sentences referred to future gains, compared with roughly three-fourths of non-AI-related" sentences, that "95% described productivity as increasing, and the share describing a decrease was negligible", and that AI's share of productivity-related sentences went from near zero pre-ChatGPT to "roughly 15% of productivity sentences" by end-2025.

Mechanism
Two-step text classification over the full transcript corpus: extract productivity-related sentences, then use an open-weight model (Qwen3-3B) to label each for AI relevance, sentiment and temporal framing. Measures what management SAYS about productivity, not what productivity did.
Why this shape
Counter-evidence, tagged to the cost ledger because the subject is productivity claims. PLACEMENT CAVEAT, stated plainly: the corpus is not cut by sector or by function, so C4 is a placement of convenience following the NBER WP 34836 precedent (logged 2026-07-30) rather than a claim that the study is about non-tech non-engineering work. Why it matters more than most entries here: it quantifies the exact failure mode this log was built to detect. A 95%-future-tense rate against a ~75% baseline for non-AI productivity talk means AI productivity discussion is roughly 5x more skewed toward the unrealized than ordinary productivity discussion is, and the near-total absence of decrease-sentences means the corpus contains almost no disconfirming management commentary at all. That is a description of narrative, not of results - and it is consistent with what this log has found by hand across six runs.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis, On the Economy, 2026-07-31
Falsifier
If the authors extend the series through 2026-2027 and the future-tense share falls materially toward the ~75% non-AI baseline, AI productivity talk will have converted from promise to reported result and this entry becomes a dated snapshot rather than a standing objection. Conversely, a flat 95% into 2027 would make it much harder to argue the log's emptiness is a timing problem.
Confidence
high — Central-bank research, full-population corpus rather than a sample, transparent method; the measured object is management language, which is exactly what it claims to measure.

2026-07-31

Assessment

No in-scope revenue evidence today. Zero S3, zero S3-CANDIDATE, zero S2, zero S1 in the main log - fourth consecutive day without new-TAM evidence from a non-tech incumbent. The day's real finding is an AI-native company volunteering that its AI does not produce revenue.

The finding that matters

Tempus AI disclosed the cleanest separation of AI capability from AI monetisation this log has recorded. Attach rate on its algorithms rose to 45% from 40% in Q1 - and the CFO immediately closed the door on the revenue reading: "Many of those algorithms remain not being paid, and so there's no impact on revenue from the number of algorithms." Meanwhile the $85m (2027) and ~$400m (2028) uplifts the company does guide to are attributed to FDA approval of tumor-only xT CDx and unified ADLT pricing - a reimbursement event, not an algorithm. Frontier capability, commercial scale, clinician pull, zero incremental revenue. Appendix scope; counter-evidence against S3.

Sources read

  • GE HealthCare (Q2 2026 call, 29 Jul) - CEO Peter Arduini: "we lead the industry in bringing AI to customers", naming Photonova Spectra, True Definition DL and Air Recon DL as AI-enabled. CFO James Saccaro cites "higher-margin AI-enabled NPIs". No AI revenue, no orders, no attach rate, no separately tracked AI metric. The single most AI-forward non-tech medtech incumbent, and AI is a margin adjective.
  • The Hartford (Q2 2026 call, 24 Jul) - CEO Chris Swift on "AI-enabled capabilities that enhance underwriting effectiveness" and underwriting completed "in a fraction of the time". Then President Morris Tooker, on the record and unprompted: "We haven't disclosed any KPIs on either the claims or the underwriting side, and we won't today." An explicit refusal, not an omission.
  • Rivian (Q2 2026 call, 30 Jul) - Autonomy+ is a genuine new SKU with no predecessor in Rivian's own catalogue, monetised since April. CEO RJ Scaringe: "take rates are trending positively." No take rate, no ARPU, no Autonomy+ revenue line. Software and services was $515m (+37%), but $308m of it is the Volkswagen JV.
  • Hippo Holdings (Q2 2026 call, 30 Jul) - logged, cost ledger. See entry.
  • Tele2 (Q2 2026 interim report, 16 Jul) - AI named as "a key pillar of Tele2's transformation strategy"; nothing quantified in any direction.
The pattern across four runs: incumbents are now fluent in AI as a margin and productivity adjective and remain unwilling to put a revenue number next to it. Hartford's Tooker is the honest version - not "we have nothing" but "we have KPIs and we are not giving them to you." That is a different and more interesting fact than absence, and it is the second week running that a large carrier has said a version of it.

Near-misses deliberately not logged

  • Bloom Energy - $1bn quarterly revenue, +165% YoY, product revenue +215%, all driven by AI data-centre demand. Fails scope, not the bar. This is selling shovels into someone else's AI capex, which METHOD excludes for the same reason it excludes chipmakers. It is the most tempting confound in the whole thesis: real, disclosed, enormous non-tech revenue caused by AI that says nothing about whether deploying AI produces revenue. Kept visible here precisely because it will keep showing up.
  • Domino Data Lab, Fifth Annual AI report (639 enterprise AI leaders; 57% say ROI still fails to outpace investment, unchanged since 2025; NA 51.1% vs UK 66.9% vs Europe 67.0%). Tier D vendor sentiment survey with no named companies and no measured outcomes. Directionally consistent with this log, which is not a reason to lower the bar for it.
  • AI-liability insurance (Corgi, Klaimee, AI Underwriting Company, Mount; Deloitte projects "nearly $5 billion global business by 2032") - genuinely new demand created by AI, but created by AI's existence, not by the insurer deploying AI. Fails test (c), the capability gate, from the deploying-company side. No premium or revenue figures disclosed by any named carrier.
  • Rivian Autonomy+ - fails the inclusion bar on the number, not on the shape. If Rivian ever discloses an Autonomy+ take rate or revenue line it is a strong S3 candidate: no predecessor SKU, and the capability gate is plausibly met.

Nearest dated catalysts

  • CMS 2027 OPPS proposed rule (proposed 2 Jul 2026) creates Software as a Medical Service - status indicator O1, 36 HCPCS codes designated, 21 into New Technology APCs. This is the reimbursement path whose absence is exactly what Tempus's CFO described. Comment period and final rule land in Q4 2026; it is the single clearest dated test of whether AI diagnostics becomes a paid category. CMS itself calls it "an interim policy rather than a permanent fix."
  • Tempus Q3 2026 - does attach rate keep climbing while payment stays at zero?
  • Deere Q3 FY26 (mid-August) - the autonomy-as-recurring-revenue disclosure this log has been waiting for; nothing new in the trailing window.
  • Caterpillar, Marriott, Hilton, Kraft Heinz and the remaining non-tech Q2 prints in the first half of August.

No findings cleared the bar for the main revenue log. 1 out-of-scope finding in the appendix and 1 in the cost ledger below.

Tempus AI Healthcare / precision diagnostics · US S1 — Velocity⚠ Counter-evidence

On the Q2 2026 call (30 Jul 2026) CFO Jim Rogers disclosed: "The algorithm attach rate in Q2 is 45%, a slight uptick from the 40% that we had quoted in Q1" - and then removed the revenue reading himself: "Many of those algorithms remain not being paid, and so there's no impact on revenue from the number of algorithms." Q2 revenue $382.5m (+22% YoY); Diagnostics $289.3m (+20%); Data and Apps $93.2m (+28%). The company's own guided uplift - "an estimated $200 uplift in ASP, which equates to approximately $85 million on an annual basis beginning in 2027" and ~$400m by 2028 - is tied to FDA approval of tumor-only xT CDx and unified ADLT pricing, i.e. to a reimbursement event, not to the algorithms.

Mechanism
AI algorithms (multi-modal models scoring pathology, imaging and molecular data) are ordered alongside a sequencing test and delivered in the report. Attach rate is the share of tests that carry at least one algorithm. The intended revenue path is per-algorithm reimbursement; that path is largely not yet open, so consumption rises while the associated revenue stays at zero.
Why this shape
S1 - the disclosed number is an input metric (attach rate), not revenue. The S1 standing caveat is satisfied in the strongest possible way: the company did not merely omit the revenue conversion, the CFO volunteered that it is zero. Logged as counter-evidence against S3: this is a frontier-capability AI product, deployed at commercial scale, consumed by clinicians on 45% of tests, generating no incremental revenue - the capability gate is passed and the newly-economic gate is not. Scope is appendix: Tempus is an AI-native diagnostics company, out of scope as a revenue-log subject; it is retained as a comparator because entrants quantify what incumbents will not, and because it is the cleanest available separation of AI capability from AI monetisation.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported

Q2 2026 earnings call transcript, 2026-07-30
Falsifier
If a later quarter shows attach rate rising further with algorithms still unpaid, the 'capability without monetisation' reading holds. It is falsified if Tempus discloses per-algorithm reimbursement revenue as a separate line, or attributes a specific dollar amount of Diagnostics revenue to algorithms rather than to ADLT/xT CDx pricing. Watch also whether the $85m 2027 and ~$400m 2028 uplifts are ever restated as algorithm-driven; today they are explicitly reimbursement-driven.
Confidence
high — Tier A full transcript; both figures and the disclaimer are direct CFO quotes, and the disclaimer cuts against the company's own interest.

Hippo Holdings Insurance · US C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering

On the Q2 2026 call (30 Jul 2026) CEO Rick McCathron said two named AI agents went live in the quarter - "Hannah, our AI service agent, and Clara, our AI First Notice of Loss agent, are both live this quarter" - and that "together they're a big part of why we can grow the top line without growing overhead at the same pace." CFO Guy Zeltser: "Our continued focus on operating leverage through AI enables us to grow our business while keeping fixed expense largely flat." Q2 revenue $144.7m; net expense ratio 45.4% (-8pp YoY); fixed expense ratio 29%, down 39 points since the start of 2024.

Mechanism
Two customer-facing LLM agents in the non-engineering cost base: Hannah handles inbound policy service contacts, Clara takes first notice of loss. Both sit where headcount would otherwise scale with policy count, so the claimed effect is a flat fixed-expense base under a growing book rather than an expense line that falls.
Why this shape
C4 - non-tech sector (a licensed home insurance carrier; revenue is premium and commission, not software) and a non-engineering function (service and claims intake). Cost ledger, not a revenue shape: management framed this as operating leverage, and no revenue is attributed to either agent. Trap 5 applies - this is cost avoidance (headcount not added), not a realized reduction, and management said so in those words. Trap 1 also present and deliberately not logged as a saving: the same call disclosed Devin, Cognition's AI software engineer, rolled out to "nearly a third of our roughly 500 employees" - an adoption metric with no outcome attached, which is why it is noted here rather than entered as a C3. The 39-point fixed-expense-ratio decline since early 2024 predates both agents and is not AI-attributable; only the Q2 flat-fixed-expense claim is.
Status
ESTIMATED — company's own internal attribution

Falsifier

It fails if fixed expense resumes scaling with the book, if the expense ratio improvement reverses, or if Hannah/Clara stop being named once the growth story needs a different explanation. Also watch whether Hippo ever quantifies the two agents' contribution rather than describing it.

Assessment

One main-log finding and it is an S1, plus one appendix entry. No S3 and no S3-CANDIDATE for the fifth consecutive run. The interesting thing today is not the finding, it is the sentence next to it: an insurance CEO describing exactly the cost-collapse-opens-a-segment mechanism this log is hunting for, and putting no premium number anywhere near it.

Sources read

  • Ategrity Specialty Q2 2026 (call 29 Jul): GWP >$205m, +22%, combined ratio 85.9%, diluted EPS >$0.60. New Architects & Engineers line launched with "approximately 60% fewer resources because of AI"; fixed cost of launching a product "all but eliminated." Logged S1 - see below.
  • ServiceNow Q2 2026 (22 Jul): AI ACV crossed $1bn, subscription revenue $3,877m +24.5%, cRPO $13.20bn, goal of AI at 30% of ACV by 2030. Logged to the appendix.
  • Aon Q2 2026 (29 Jul): revenue $4.25bn, organic +5%. Claims Copilot named Innovation of the Year; the analyzers described as "revenue-generating engines on behalf of clients"; "AI accelerates what we've been working on." Not one number attributed. A broker whose entire product is analytics, calling its analytics revenue-generating, and declining to size it.
  • ADP Q4 FY2026 (29 Jul): FY revenue $21.9bn +7%, ES new business bookings $2.2bn +6%. ADP Assist at 3.1m active users and 12m conversations; Lyric HCM live clients +94%, pipeline +50%, 70% of opportunities from new logos. CEO Black: "Every single one of those conversations was anchored in an AI discussion." CFO Hadley on AI: "allowed us to stay flat while investing in growth areas" - i.e. cost. No AI dollars in either revenue or bookings.
  • L'Oreal H1 2026 (29 Jul): sales EUR 23.77bn, +6.8% like-for-like, e-commerce growing at nearly twice the market. A "landmark collaboration" with OpenAI announced and 8,000+ digital/tech/data staff cited. "AI will help it maintain its pace" - future tense, zero attribution, in the results release of the company with the loudest beauty-tech narrative in the sector.
  • Nestle H1 2026 (23 Jul): sales CHF 43,109m, organic 3.6%, RIG 1.5%, e-commerce 21.8% of group sales. Zero mentions of AI or machine learning in the release. Second time in four runs a global top-five consumer or pharma name has published a major print without using the word (AstraZeneca, 27 Jul).
  • Frontier Group Q2 2026 (29 Jul): record revenue $1.28bn, +38%. Credits "more disciplined revenue management" and better pricing tools - and contains no mention of AI, artificial intelligence or machine learning at all. An airline improving yield with pricing technology and pointedly not calling it AI.
  • Quest Diagnostics Q2 2026 (23 Jul): revenue $3.04bn, +10.2%. AI located explicitly inside the Invigorate cost-savings program - "driving continuous improvements in quality and productivity." Cost-side by the company's own framing.
The pattern hardens and now has a name: the attribution gap is not shy, it is structural. Five runs in, the tape splits three ways with almost no overlap. Non-tech incumbents talk about AI and attribute nothing (Aon, ADP, L'Oreal, Ategrity). Non-tech incumbents post excellent revenue numbers and attribute them to discipline, pricing and mix rather than AI (Frontier +38%, Quest +10.2%, GM's OnStar line on 28 Jul). And the only parties putting large, auditable AI revenue figures on the record are the vendors selling it - ServiceNow $1bn of AI ACV, Infosys 8.2% of revenue. Someone's $1bn is someone else's unattributed cost line.

Near-misses deliberately not logged

  • Aon - fails inclusion bar #2. Executive language about revenue generation with no figure and no unit is not an on-the-record statement of material revenue impact; it is positioning.
  • ADP - the Lyric metrics (+94% live clients, +50% pipeline) are genuine S1-shaped input metrics on a revenue-producing process, but they are attributed to a product, not to AI, and ADP's own AI framing is explicitly cost ("stay flat"). Subject status is borderline anyway - GICS moved ADP to Industrials, but it sells HCM software.
  • L'Oreal x OpenAI - an announcement with no result. METHOD rejects these by name, and it does not matter how large the logo is.
  • Frontier Group - nothing to attribute. Recording it as a source precisely because the absence is the data: +38% revenue growth from revenue-management tooling that management declines to label AI. The reverse of attribution laundering, and a useful reminder that the AI label is a choice.
  • Schneider Electric - record revenue on data-centre demand. Same rejection as Swiss Re on 27 Jul: selling into the buildout is a shovel, not a result. This will keep recurring and will keep being rejected.
  • Grant Thornton manufacturing cut, resurfaced via Forbes (9 Jul) - 0 of 100 manufacturing leaders reporting significant revenue increase from AI. Already logged 27 Jul; a second write-up of the same survey is a duplicate claim, not a new finding.
  • MIT Project NANDA ~5% of integrated pilots capturing real value, cited in the same Forbes piece - no named company, and the study is already effectively represented by the MIT FutureTech entry logged 26 Jul.

Nearest dated catalysts

  • CVS Health Q2 2026, 31 Jul - the largest healthcare print left this week.
  • Caterpillar Q2 2026, early Aug - the CES 2026 autonomy launch has had two quarters to produce a revenue line. If autonomy is a new subscription SKU it should be sized by now.
  • Deere Q3 FY2026, mid-Aug - precision-ag recurring revenue and digital attach rates. Deere has a stated recurring-revenue target that autonomy is supposed to carry; no actuals disclosed as of the Feb coverage reviewed today.
  • Marriott, Booking, McDonald's, Yum early Aug - the personalization and dynamic-pricing cohort, where S2 evidence should appear first if it appears at all.
  • Ategrity Q3 2026, late Oct - the falsifier check. Does any premium ever get attached to the classes launched under the collapsed launch cost?
  • ServiceNow Q3 2026, late Oct - whether AI ACV keeps compounding toward the 2030 goal or the metric goes quiet.

Cost ledger opened - 15 backfilled entries

A second ledger was added today, at Tim's direction, after five consecutive runs produced almost no revenue-attributed evidence. Cost savings never become S1, S2 or S3; they are counted separately and are rendered in their own section. The rationale and the containment rules are in METHOD.md. The buckets are a 2x2 - sector (tech / non-tech) x function (software engineering / everything else) - because the cell comparison is the whole point.

The backfill's headline: across 15 entries, exactly four companies have ever attached a dollar figure to an AI cost saving, and the only dollar-quantified engineering saving in existence is Amazon's $260m from August 2024, which nobody - Amazon included - has repeated in two years. Everything else in the engineering cells is an adoption or productivity percentage.

What the 2x2 shows on day one

  • C1 tech / engineering - Meta +30% output per engineer (+80% power users), Google 75% of new code AI-generated, Amazon $260m / 4,500 developer-years. Big numbers, one dollar figure, and it is two years old.
  • C2 tech / non-engineering - Snap >$500m annualized cost base in an SEC 8-K, IBM $4.5bn productivity savings, Salesforce $100m out of support. The dollars live here. So do the definitional problems.
  • C3 non-tech / engineering - Amex 30-40% coding cycle time, BNY 40% of code AI-written, BofA >20% developer productivity across ~19k developers. Not one dollar between them.
  • C4 non-tech / non-engineering - DBS ~S$1bn economic value, Santander EUR 35m in Q1 rising to a EUR 1bn 2028 target, JPMorgan 30-40% job reductions in discrete areas, UnitedHealth 69,000 administrative hours. The cell that matters, and the one where the metrics are softest.
The pattern across the cost ledger mirrors the revenue log exactly: the closer a disclosure gets to an audited financial statement, the vaguer the AI attribution becomes. Snap put the AI framing and the $500m in the same 8-K - and attributed the money to "these changes," not to AI. Salesforce's $100m exists in an interview and has never appeared in a filing. Telstra delivered A$181m of labour savings and its CEO declined to name a single role AI replaced.

Corrections to prior days, found while verifying

Verification was run before logging; three prior-day readings did not survive it.

  • Bank of America - the >20% developer-productivity figure was attributed to Moynihan on the call in the 26 Jul record. It is a slide-20 bullet in the official presentation materials; his spoken version is qualitative. Still Tier A, but not a quote.
  • BNY - the 28 Jul record dated the call 22 Jul (a publication date; the call was 15 Jul) and attributed "capacity creator" to CFO McDonogh. It is CEO Robin Vince, in Q&A.
  • American Express - call date is 24 Jul, not the 21 Jul implied earlier; and Squeri's 30-40% is followed immediately by "Now that's really not a savings because what that does is allows us to do more" - a disclaimer the earlier summary omitted, which changes what the figure means.

Cost claims checked and deliberately NOT logged

  • Microsoft $500m call-centre saving - traces to reporting of an internal presentation; no verbatim obtainable from any source. Microsoft's own Q3 FY26 transcript contains no quantified internal AI saving.
  • Klarna $40m / 700 agents - a 2024 projection, subsequently walked back, and the "700 agents" was always a company estimate rather than a headcount cut.
  • Ahold Delhaize EUR 5bn and Nestle's 16,000 job cuts - broad cost-out and restructuring programmes with no company AI attribution.
  • IKEA / Ingka EUR 1.3bn from Billie - that is revenue from remote sales, not a cost saving; Ingka states its 2026 layoffs were not AI-attributed.
  • Verizon $200m energy saving - real and company-sourced, but network energy optimisation, and arguably an engineering-adjacent cost rather than either function cell. Held for a future run.
  • Coinbase, Anthropic, Nvidia coding claims - unnamed spokespeople, personal rather than company-level figures, or purely directional.
  • CBA reversing 45 AI-driven redundancies (Aug 2025) and the McDonald's / Taco Bell voice-AI reversals - genuine walk-backs, but deployment reversals without a retracted savings figure. Candidates if a number surfaces.
Ategrity Specialty Insurance Company Holdings Insurance · US S1 — Velocity

On the Q2 2026 call (29 Jul 2026) management said the new Architects & Engineers product was "brought to market earlier this month using what we estimate to be approximately 60% fewer resources because of AI," and that the company has "all but eliminated" the fixed cost of launching a new product. Q2 gross written premium was more than $205m, up 22%+, with no premium attributed to AI or broken out for the new lines.

Mechanism
Product-launch capacity, not underwriting. Ategrity uses AI (including agentic tooling across marketing and governance) to build and file a new specialty line - forms, rating, appetite, distribution collateral - with roughly 60% of the previous internal resource. The revenue path is that a carrier which previously could only afford to stand up large classes can now stand up smaller ones, so the addressable set of classes widens. The premium consequence is entirely undisclosed.
Why this shape
S1, not S3, and the distinction is the whole point. The disclosed number is an input metric (resources consumed to launch), never revenue. On the S3 tests: (a) counterfactual purchase fails - an architects-and-engineers buyer would have bought A&E cover from one of many existing carriers; (b) prior alternative fails for the same reason, this is an old, competitively written class, so any premium won here is taken from a competitor; (c) capability gate is arguable at best - form drafting and filing support does not obviously require a post-2024 frontier model; (d) newly economic is the only test with any pull, and only for Ategrity's own economics, not for the segment. Revenue conversion was conspicuously NOT disclosed: the 10 Jul pre-announcement says "recently launched products... began contributing meaningfully" and attaches no dollar figure, and the call attributes none of the 22%+ premium growth to AI. Note also that this is the closest any incumbent in this log has come to articulating the METHOD cost-collapse-opens-a-segment mechanism in its own words - "all but eliminated the fixed cost of launching a new product" is the S3 pathway stated without the S3 evidence.

Falsifier

Specific check: does Ategrity ever name a class it wrote only because the launch cost fell?

Alphabet / Google Technology - internet platform · US C1 — Tech · engineering

CEO Sundar Pichai, at Google Cloud Next (April 2026): "Today, 75% of all new code at Google is now AI-generated and approved by engineers, up from 50% last fall." On the same stage: "a particularly complex code migration done by agents and engineers working together was completed six times faster than was possible a year ago with engineers alone." In his Q2 2026 earnings remarks (22 Jul 2026) he added that "a team in Chrome is now on track to accelerate delivery by eight times, compressing a two-year timeline into three months through model-driven refactoring."

Mechanism
AI code generation and agentic refactoring across Google's engineering organisation, with the migration and Chrome examples given as elapsed-time compression on specific projects.
Why this shape
C1. The 75% is a pure ADOPTION metric (METHOD trap 1) and is the single most misquoted number in this field - the wording is 75% of all NEW code, not 75% of Google's code, and Pichai attaches no cost saving, no headcount effect and no dollar to it. The 6x and 8x figures are project-level elapsed-time claims, not a cost line. Logged because the trajectory (50% -> 75% in roughly two quarters) is real information about engineering practice, and because the absence of any cost figure behind the most-cited adoption statistic in the industry is itself the finding.

Falsifier
Alphabet has never converted any of these into an expense number. If AI-generated code share saturates near 100% with no disclosed effect on engineering cost, headcount or shipping cadence in the financials, the metric was never about cost. Watch for a first cost or headcount attribution in a 10-K or on a call rather than a keynote.
Confidence
medium — Tier B company blog, verbatim and on-the-record from the CEO - but a keynote, not a filing, and an adoption metric rather than a result.
Amazon Technology - internet platform · US C1 — Tech · engineering

On the Q2 2024 call (1 Aug 2024) CEO Andy Jassy said: "With Q's code transformation capabilities, Amazon has migrated over 30,000 Java JDK applications in a few months, saving the company $260 million and 4,500 developer years compared to what it would have otherwise cost." Logged 2026-07-30 as a deliberately dated benchmark: it remains the only dollar-quantified AI engineering saving found from any company, and Amazon's April 2026 shareholder letter contains no comparable figure.

Mechanism
Automated Java version-migration across ~30,000 applications, priced as the labour that would otherwise have been required. Explicitly counterfactual - "compared to what it would have otherwise cost" - so it is cost avoidance on work Amazon chose to do, not a reduction in an existing expense line.
Why this shape
C1, and logged with its age stated up front because the age is the point. This figure is two years old, it is the only engineering-function dollar figure anyone has published, and no company has repeated the trick since - including Amazon. That is a strong signal about how hard it is to convert coding productivity into an auditable number: the one clean case was a mechanical, countable migration with an obvious counterfactual, which is exactly the kind of work that does NOT generalise to feature development. METHOD trap 5 applies: this is cost avoidance, not realized reduction.
Status
ESTIMATED — company's own internal attribution
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Q2 2024 earnings call (transcript), 2024-08-01
Falsifier
Already partly falsified as a general claim by two years of silence. If no company - Amazon included - publishes a comparable engineering-function dollar figure by end-2026 despite adoption metrics at 40-75%, then coding productivity is structurally unbankable and every C1/C3 percentage should be discounted accordingly.
Confidence
high — Tier A transcript, named CEO, explicit counterfactual framing. Confidence is in the quote, not in its currency - the figure is deliberately old and flagged as such.
Snap Technology - internet platform · US C2 — Tech · non-engineering

In an SEC Form 8-K dated 15 Apr 2026, CEO Evan Spiegel told employees Snap would cut "approximately 1,000 team members at Snap, including 16% of our full time employees, in addition to closing more than 300 open roles," and that "we expect to reduce our annualized cost base by more than $500 million by the second half of 2026." The accompanying investor update is headed "AI-Driven Transformation"; adjusted OpEx guidance was cut ~$250m to ~$2.75bn and SBC ~$150m to ~$1.05bn.

Mechanism
Workforce reduction concentrated outside engineering, with the stated rationale that "rapid advancements in artificial intelligence enable our teams to reduce repetitive work, increase velocity, and better support our community, partners, and advertisers." The investor update cites a support agent answering "1M+ questions/month" and an AI code-review agent finding "7,500+ bugs."
Why this shape
C2, and the highest-grade source in this ledger - an SEC filing with a quantified Item 2.05 charge ($95-130m pre-tax), not a press release. But METHOD trap 4 applies hard and must not be smoothed: Spiegel attributes the >$500m to "these changes" broadly - prioritisation and restructuring - while the investor deck FRAMES the programme as AI-driven. Snap does not state that AI caused $500m of savings, and this entry must not be read as "AI saved Snap $500m." What Snap did do, uniquely, is put the AI rationale and the dollar number in the same filed document, which is why it is logged. Status PROJECTED: the $500m is estimated on the second half of 2026 and had not been realized at filing.
Status
PROJECTED — forward target, not a result
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Form 8-K, Exhibits 99.1 and 99.2 (Item 2.05), 2026-04-15
Falsifier
Adjusted OpEx of ~$2.75bn and SBC of ~$1.05bn are hard, checkable numbers. If FY2026 lands at or above the prior ~$3.0bn / ~$1.2bn, the saving did not materialise. Second test: whether headcount stays down through 2027 or quietly rebuilds, which is what happened to the canonical Klarna case.
Confidence
high — SEC filing, verbatim, with checkable guidance revisions. The uncertainty is in AI's causal share, not in the numbers - and that uncertainty is stated above rather than papered over.
IBM Technology - IT services and software · US C2 — Tech · non-engineering

On the Q1 2026 call (22 Apr 2026) CFO James Kavanaugh said AI-driven internal transformation has "driven $4.5 billion of productivity savings, with an additional $1 billion expected in 2026." On the Q4 2025 call CEO Arvind Krishna described the same figure differently - "exiting 2025 with $4.5 billion of annual run rate savings" rising to "$5.5 billion by the end of next year" - and said "Our entire developer workforce is using Bob with average productivity gains of 45%."

Mechanism
Enterprise-wide AI deployment across HR, customer support, procurement, finance and IT operations. IBM's own function-level detail: $165m of operational savings in customer support since 2022, $600m in enterprise IT since 2022, and 94% of common employee inquiries answered by AskHR without human intervention.
Why this shape
C2 - largest dollar figure in this ledger, and logged with its internal inconsistency exposed rather than resolved. Krishna calls the $4.5bn "annual run rate savings" exiting 2025; Kavanaugh calls it "productivity savings since 2023," i.e. cumulative. These are materially different claims about the same number and IBM has not reconciled them. METHOD trap 3 also applies: "productivity savings" is IBM's own non-GAAP construct, not a GAAP expense line. The 45% Project Bob developer figure is a C1-shaped claim inside the same company; not split into a second entry, per the one-disclosure-one-ledger rule, but noted here because IBM is the only company claiming both an engineering percentage and an enterprise dollar.
Status
ESTIMATED — company's own internal attribution
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Q1 2026 earnings call + Q4 2025 earnings call (transcripts), 2026-04-22
Falsifier
The reconciliation is the test. If the $4.5bn is a run rate, IBM's expense base should show roughly that much structural reduction against 2023; if it is cumulative since 2023, the annual figure is far smaller than the headline implies. Watch whether the number is ever tied to a GAAP line, and whether the promised additional $1bn in 2026 shows up in gross margin or SG&A.
Confidence
medium — Tier A transcripts and verbatim quotes, but two mutually inconsistent framings of the same figure from CEO and CFO, on a company-defined non-GAAP metric.
Salesforce Technology - software · US C2 — Tech · non-engineering

EVP and Chief Digital Officer Joe Inzerillo, in an interview published 3 Oct 2025: "We've been able to take about $100 million in cost out of our support function and deliver as good or better CSAT for our customers." CEO Marc Benioff, on a podcast reported 2 Sep 2025, on support headcount: "I've reduced it from 9,000 heads to about 5,000, because I need less heads." Neither figure appears in any Salesforce earnings call or SEC filing - verified against the Q4 FY26 (25 Feb 2026) and Q1 FY27 (27 May 2026) official transcripts, which contain no support-cost or support-headcount figure at all.

Mechanism
Agentforce deployed on Salesforce's own support organisation - deflection and autonomous resolution of inbound cases, with an SDR agent additionally reported to have processed 43,000+ leads generating $1.7m of pipeline and a service agent handling 1.4m+ conversations.
Why this shape
C2, logged at Tier C on purpose, and the disclosure gap is the finding. This is the single most-cited enterprise AI cost-savings claim in circulation, and it exists only in an interview and a podcast - never in a filing, never on an earnings call, never in a document management is legally exposed on. METHOD's tier logic says exactly why that matters. Two further cautions: the widely repeated Bloomberg/Dreamforce version attributing the $100m to Benioff has no locatable verbatim, so the dollar claim is credited here to Inzerillo, who did say it on the record; and the only 2026 first-party number in this area is a productivity-hours metric - Robin Washington, Q1 FY27: Slackbot is "driving 3.8 million hours of annualized productivity gains" - which Salesforce never converts to dollars.
Status
ESTIMATED — company's own internal attribution
Source
Tier C — Third-party reporting — journalism, trade press, sell-side
CIO interview with Joe Inzerillo (EVP/CDO); Fortune report of Benioff podcast remarks, 2025-10-03
Falsifier
Salesforce reports segment margins quarterly. If ~$100m came out of support and 4,000 support roles went, the effect should be visible in cost of revenue and in total headcount - and management should eventually be willing to say so on a call. Continued absence from filings while the number circulates in the press is itself the falsification: an unaudited claim no executive will repeat under securities liability.
Confidence
low — Named executives and verbatim quotes, but Tier C sources, one of them a podcast, and both conspicuously absent from every first-party financial disclosure since.
American Express Financial services · US C3 — Non-tech · engineering

On the Q2 2026 call (24 Jul 2026) CEO Stephen Squeri said AI has produced "anywhere from a 30 to 40% decrease in cycle time from a coding perspective" - and immediately disclaimed it as a saving: "Now that's really not a savings because what that does is allows us to do more." No dollar amount, no headcount reduction, no AI-attributed expense figure anywhere on the call.

Mechanism
AI coding assistance inside Amex's own technology organisation, compressing the elapsed time from requirement to shipped code. Squeri names technology as "one of the first places that we have deployed it." The gain is explicitly converted into throughput, not into a smaller technology budget.
Why this shape
C3 - non-tech subject, software engineering function. The most useful thing here is the disclaimer. Trap 2 in METHOD: a CEO volunteering that a productivity gain is reinvested rather than banked. CFO Le Caillec guided operating expense to "mid-single digits" for the year and said "AI is going to play a critical role in that" - with no AI-specific figure attached, so the expense guide cannot be credited to AI. Servicing headcount discussed qualitatively only: hiring "really hasn't grown and we would expect over time for that number to decrease." Verified against two independent transcripts; the widely circulated phrasing "AI is already reducing coding cycle times by 30% to 40%" is a paraphrase and is not quoted here.
Status
ESTIMATED — company's own internal attribution
Source

Falsifier
If Amex's technology expense line, or total operating expense growth, never bends below the mid-single-digit trend while the coding-cycle claim keeps being repeated, then 30-40% faster coding is a throughput story with no P&L consequence - exactly as Squeri said it was. Watch whether any later call converts it into a dollar or a headcount.

BNY Financial services · US C3 — Non-tech · engineering

On the Q2 2026 call (15 Jul 2026) CFO Dermot McDonogh said that of "our engineers at BNY, roughly 40% of the software written is now written using AI." CEO Robin Vince declined to quantify any benefit - "We don't break out the very specific economic numbers" - and McDonogh de-attributed the headcount decline: "It's down 7% over the year, but that's as a result of everything."

Mechanism
AI coding tools across BNY's engineering organisation, inside an engineering budget management sizes at "approximately $4 billion" - within which McDonogh describes AI spend as "quite de minimis and quite modest."
Why this shape
C3, and a textbook instance of METHOD trap 1: 40% is an ADOPTION metric, not a cost saving. Nothing follows from it about expense. Vince frames the output as capacity rather than savings - "It would be a mistake to think about this capacity as just an efficiency creator" - and when Mike Mayo asked directly for expense-savings or revenue numbers, Vince answered "it's not that we can't identify benefits, we certainly can. It's that we recognize that all of these things coming together are ultimately the success." The only nearby dollar figures - ~$500mm incremental investment and ~$450mm of 2026 efficiency savings - are firmwide and not AI-attributed. Note the correction to this log's own earlier reading: the 27 Jul day record placed this call on 22 Jul (a publication date) and attributed "capacity creator" to the CFO; it is Vince, in Q&A, on 15 Jul.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Q2 2026 earnings call (transcript), 2026-07-15
Falsifier
BNY has now twice declined to quantify. If the ~$450mm efficiency line is delivered and management still will not attribute any share of it to AI, then AI at BNY is a capability programme, not a cost programme, and the 40% is a vanity metric. Conversely a first AI-attributed dollar in any 2027 disclosure would be a genuine update.
Confidence
high — Two independent transcripts agree on every quote; the refusal to quantify is explicit and repeated, which makes it hard to misread.
Bank of America Financial services · US C3 — Non-tech · engineering

Bank of America's official Q2 2026 presentation materials (call 14 Jul 2026, slide 20) disclose "~19K developers leveraging real-time coding assistance, increasing productivity >20%," alongside ~200K active users generating ">400K prompts per day" and ">300 AI / machine learning use cases approved | 114 live generative AI use cases with 34 fully deployed." No dollar saving and no AI-attributed headcount figure was given.

Mechanism
Coding assistance across ~19,000 developers, plus AI embedded in operations, risk, finance and client-facing workflows. Moynihan's framing is capacity, not cost: "the same amount of money in '27 will get us more code for lack of a better term, in '28."
Why this shape
C3. Correction to this log's 26 Jul day record, which attributed ">20% developer productivity" to Moynihan on the call: the figure appears only as a slide-20 bullet in the official presentation materials. Moynihan's spoken version is qualitative - "Our developers code more efficiently." Still Tier A, since the deck is a filed investor document, but the attribution matters. Moynihan does claim economics for the portfolio - "over 300 AI use cases approved - all of which have good economics" - without disclosing any of them. DBS Group Banking · Singapore C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering

DBS's Annual Report 2025 (published ~8 Mar 2026) states the bank has "over 2,000 models and more than 430 use cases delivering economic value of approximately SGD 1 billion." The trajectory is on record: a year earlier DBS's head of generative AI said the bank "has earned S$750 million through its use of the technology" and expected economic value "could surpass S$1 billion this year."

Mechanism
Roughly 430 production use cases across customer operations, credit decisioning, wealth prospecting and process automation, with the bank's own attribution model converting each to an economic-value figure.
Why this shape
C4 - the cell that matters, and the largest number in it. But METHOD trap 3 applies squarely: "economic value" is a DBS-defined composite of revenue uplift, cost avoidance and loss avoidance, and DBS publishes neither the methodology nor a cost-only split. So this cannot be presented as a S$1bn cost saving; it is a S$1bn internally-attributed benefit of unknown composition. It is logged at Tier A because it appears in an audited annual report over the Chairman and CEO's names, which is a materially higher bar than the interview-grade sourcing behind most claims of this size, and because the disclosed year-on-year progression (S$750m to ~S$1bn) is the kind of series that can be tracked and falsified.
Status
ESTIMATED — company's own internal attribution
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
DBS Annual Report 2025, Letter from Chairman and CEO, 2026-03-08
Falsifier
Two tests. First, whether DBS ever discloses the cost/revenue split or the attribution method - continued refusal after several years of headline growth suggests a metric built for the annual report. Second, whether the bank's cost-to-income ratio actually improves in line with a benefit of this size relative to its expense base; if economic value compounds while cost-income is flat, the metric is not reaching the P&L.
Confidence
medium — Annual report and verbatim, but an undefined composite metric with no published methodology and no cost-only figure.
Banco Santander Banking · Spain C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering

Santander disclosed (22 Jun 2026) "EUR 35 million in business value generated in Q1" from AI, expected to "exceed EUR 200 million by year-end," against an Investor Day (25 Feb 2026) target of "more than EUR 1 billion of business value annually (cost savings plus revenues) from data and AI initiatives" by 2028, "contributing around 1 percentage point of the group's cost-to-income improvement." Operational detail: a Brazilian card-fraud claims process made "around 95% faster, with up to 90% automation and an error rate below 1%."

Mechanism
Contact-centre self-service, fraud-claims automation and AML alert processing across a 185,000-employee base. Named targets include ~240,000 UK voice calls (40% of annual volume) resolved through self-service, giving "service teams around 45,000 hours back," and Openbank AI models processing ~100,000 AML alerts per year.
Why this shape
C4. Trap 3 again, and Santander is at least explicit about it - "business value" is defined in its own words as "cost savings plus revenues," so the cost share of the EUR 35m and the EUR 1bn is unknown. Logged because of one unusually falsifiable feature: Santander tied the programme to a specific, auditable financial target - about 1 percentage point of cost-to-income improvement - which almost no company does. That converts a soft claim into a checkable one. The widely reported "EUR 500m+ cost reduction / GBP 433m" split appears only in wire coverage, not in Santander's own text, and is deliberately excluded here.
Status
ESTIMATED — company's own internal attribution
Source

Santander corporate news page + 2026 Investor Day press release, 2026-06-22
Falsifier
The cost-to-income ratio. A ~1pp improvement attributed to data and AI by 2028 is a hard, published, auditable number - if the ratio does not move by roughly that much, or moves for unrelated reasons management then credits to AI, the claim fails. Interim test: does the promised EUR 200m+ for FY26 get confirmed with a figure in the annual report rather than a corporate news page?
Confidence
medium — Company-issued but Tier B (story page plus Investor Day release, not a filing), and a composite metric - offset by an unusually specific and checkable cost-to-income commitment.
JPMorganChase Banking · US C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering

On the Q2 2026 call (14 Jul 2026) Jamie Dimon said: "We have had discrete areas where we did reduce jobs by 30% or 40%, and most of those people offered jobs elsewhere," alongside "almost 1,000 use cases today." At the 2025 Investor Day, Marianne Lake guided that "headcount will trend down by about 10% over the next five years or so, even as the business grows by another more than 25%," and Jeremy Barnum said "our investments in AI/ML delivered a 35% increase in value last year." No dollar figure, no named units, and no absolute headcount were disclosed.

Mechanism
Roughly 1,000 use cases with about 50 described as material, across risk, fraud, marketing, hedging, prospecting, note-taking, idea generation and document reading. The headcount effect is concentrated in operations, where Lake noted ops headcount grew only 13% over five years against 25%+ business growth.
Why this shape
C4, and the most concrete headcount claim from any non-tech incumbent found so far: a named CEO stating realized 30-40% job reductions in specific (unnamed) areas. Two hard caveats. Barnum's "35% increase in value" is a growth rate off an undisclosed base and is NOT a dollar figure - the $1-1.5bn and $2bn AI-value numbers circulating for JPMorgan did not appear in any 2026 primary document checked and are excluded. And Dimon in the same breath argues against the durable-margin thesis this ledger might otherwise be read as supporting: "the ultimate beneficiary of AI will be our customers," "the benefit accrues to the customer, not to JPMorgan," "AI will have its gives and takes. We can't project." That is the CEO of the largest US bank predicting AI cost gains are competed away into price - the single most important qualification in this ledger.
Status

Falsifier
The -10% headcount-over-five-years guide is the falsifier, and it is public and dated. If total headcount is flat or up by 2030 while the business grows, the operations reduction was reallocation rather than structural. Dimon's own competed-away thesis predicts exactly that outcome in the financials: efficiency delivered to customers, not to the expense line.

UnitedHealth Group Healthcare - payer and services · US C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering

On the Q2 2026 call (16 Jul 2026) UnitedHealth disclosed that AI has "year to date... processed about half a million prior auths and saved 69,000 administrative hours," with "96% first-pass approval for digital prior authorizations." Optum's Patrick Conway said ambient listening is "available to 70% of our employed providers today and on track to exceed 90% by year-end" and that "patient access has expanded by nearly 200,000 more patient-facing hours." Forward commitments include $3bn of AI investment through 2027 and eliminating 30% of total prior-auth volume by end-2026.

Mechanism
AI adjudication of prior authorisations at Optum Insight, plus ambient clinical documentation across employed providers - the two highest-volume administrative processes in US healthcare.
Why this shape
C4, and structurally the most interesting entry in this ledger because the metric is hours, not dollars, and UnitedHealth offers no cost-per-hour conversion. 69,000 hours is roughly 33 FTE-years - against a company of ~400,000 people, which is why the honest read is that this is an early-innings figure and not yet a margin event. Logged anyway because prior authorisation is exactly the kind of high-volume, rule-bound administrative work where AI cost effects should appear first and most measurably, so the series is worth tracking from a low base. The ambient-listening figure is capacity creation (more patient-facing hours), i.e. C4-shaped input, not a saving.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Q2 2026 earnings call (transcript), 2026-07-16

The 30%-of-prior-auth-volume-eliminated-by-end-2026 target is dated, specific and checkable. Confidence

NBER Working Paper 34836 - 'Firm Data on AI' (~6,000 executives, 4 countries) Cross-sector study · US / UK / Germany / Japan C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering⚠ Counter-evidence

Survey of ~6,000 executives across four countries, fielded Nov 2025 - Jan 2026: "More than 90 percent of executives report no effect of AI use on employment over the past three years" and "89 percent report no impact on labor productivity." Among the minority reporting an effect, the "estimated average boost of 0.29 percent." Forward expectations are also small: AI "will increase labor productivity by 1.4 percent on average while reducing employment by 0.7 percent."

Mechanism
Firm-level executive survey measuring realized effects on employment and labour productivity over a three-year window, rather than sentiment about AI's promise.
Why this shape
C4 as the modal respondent (non-tech, non-engineering), though the finding spans all four cells and should be read against every one of them. This is the most important entry in the ledger and it cuts against it: at the population level, the effects this ledger records at named large firms are not visible. Distinguished from the consultant surveys METHOD rejects because it measures realized outcomes rather than expectations, is authored by identifiable academic economists, and reports its own null result. The obvious reconciliation - and the load-bearing assumption to interrogate - is selection: the log records the small tail of firms that both achieved something and chose to publish it. Co-author Gregory Thwaites: "80% of the time, neither of those things seem to have changed much... But the answer is very much 'yet.'"
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier C — Third-party reporting — journalism, trade press, sell-side
NBER Working Paper 34836 (authors incl. Yotzov, Barrero, Bloom, Bunn, Davis, Mizen, Thwaites), via NBER Digest, 2026-05-01
Falsifier
A later wave of the same instrument showing the no-effect share falling materially below 90%. That is the cleanest available test of whether the tail is spreading to the mean or staying a tail - and it is the number that would change this log's overall verdict more than any single corporate disclosure.
Confidence
high — Large multi-country sample, credible authorship, measures realized rather than anticipated effects, and reports a null - the least incentive-compatible result to publish.
Telstra Group Telecom · Australia C4 — Non-tech · non-engineering⚠ Counter-evidence

At its 1H FY26 results briefing (19 Feb 2026) Telstra reported underlying labour costs down 9% (-A$181m), more than 1,000 roles cut in the half and 2,356 in CY2025, with headcount down 7% - and CEO Vicki Brady "could not point to a specific role that AI has directly replaced so far." CFO Michael Ackland: "There is a risk here that you end up in software licencing, cloud cost and in paying the AI providers so that you offset your benefits."

Mechanism
Large realized labour-cost reduction delivered by restructuring, in a company simultaneously running a substantial AI programme - and explicitly declining to connect the two.
Why this shape
C4 counter-evidence, and the cleanest example in either ledger of the pattern this log keeps meeting from the other direction: real savings, real AI programme, and management refusing to join them. It is the cost-side twin of UPS (logged 28 Jul), which attributed A$-scale transformation benefits to network reconfiguration with no AI attribution anywhere. Ackland's warning is the run-cost offset (METHOD trap 6) stated by a CFO on the record, and group executive Kim Krogh Andersen put it more sharply: "we will actually see the run cost of AI outperform the benefits of AI" without the right foundation. Any AI cost thesis has to price this objection; almost no vendor case study does.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier C — Third-party reporting — journalism, trade press, sell-side
Results briefing coverage (iTnews, CFOtech) of 1H FY26 results, 2026-02-19
Falsifier
If a future Telstra result does attribute a labour or cost line to AI with a figure, this entry weakens. If successive halves keep delivering labour savings that management keeps declining to attribute, it strengthens - and the reasonable inference becomes that AI-labelled savings elsewhere in this ledger are partly relabelled restructuring.
Confidence
medium — Tier C trade-press reporting of a real results briefing with named executives quoted; the underlying A$181m and headcount figures are company-reported, the AI-attribution refusal is directly quoted.

2026-07-29

permalink →

Assessment

Zero findings in the main log for the second day running. No S3, no S3-CANDIDATE, and no non-tech corporate anywhere on the 27-29 Jul tape put a revenue number next to AI. One appendix entry, and it is the most useful thing found this run precisely because it cuts against the thesis: Infosys disclosed AI revenue rising and total growth guidance falling in the same release.

Sources read

  • Infosys Q1 FY27 (23 Jul): 8.2% of revenue is AI-first services on $5,082m (derived ~$417m), up from ~5.5% two quarters earlier - and FY27 constant-currency growth guidance narrowed down to 1.5-3.0% from 1.5-3.5% in the same release. Logged to the appendix. See below.
  • IQVIA Q2 2026 (28 Jul): revenue $4.368bn +8.7%, R&D net new bookings $3.15bn +19.3%, book-to-bill 1.22, backlog $34.2bn. AI content is entirely adoption counting - 294 agents across 90 use cases, 19 of the top 20 pharma have deployed IQVIA AI tools, 4 of the top 10 contracted to co-develop. Not one dollar of the record bookings is attributed to any of it. CFO Fedock: AI is "just another lever in that toolkit."
  • Ford Q2 2026 (28 Jul): revenue $48.3bn; 1.6m paid subscriptions, BlueCruise paid subs +20%, Ford Pro Intelligence >900k +20%, BlueCruise 50% of retail Integrated Services revenue. Farley calls it "our best proof point for software." Not called AI, and correctly so - see near-misses.
  • Philips Q2 2026 (28 Jul): group sales EUR 4.4bn, +4% comparable. Several AI-powered clearances in the quarter, SmartIQ cited at >50% less radiation. No AI revenue or order-intake breakout of any kind.
  • Hilton Q2 2026 (28 Jul): RevPAR +3.9%, adjusted EBITDA $1.054bn. Exactly one AI mention on the call and it is about other people's datacentre capex as a source of travel demand. Nothing attributed.
  • AstraZeneca Q2 2026 (27 Jul): zero mentions of AI or machine learning in the entire transcript. A top-five pharma, mid-AI-boom, discussing pipeline and timelines for an hour without using the word. Worth recording as its own data point.
  • T-Mobile Q2 2026 (23 Jul): service revenue +9%, postpaid churn 0.85%. First network-native AI application (live translation) shipped in beta; Dynamic CX ran during the World Cup. Framed explicitly as future opportunity, no revenue quantified.
  • Kinsale Capital Q2 2026 (24 Jul): combined ratio 75.5%, operating ROE 24.4%. CFO frames AI as efficiency that "could offset" rising reinsurance commissions - cost side, out of scope by construction.
  • Goosehead Insurance Q2 2026 (22 Jul): revenue $113m +21%, written premium $1.36bn +14%. Digital Agent 2.0 launched in Texas and described as "the country's first true end-to-end choice shopping platform for personal lines" - with no premium or revenue contribution disclosed and, on the read obtained, no use of the term AI at all.
  • Publicis Groupe H1 2026 (16 Jul): Q2 organic +4.8%, Q1 +4.5%, FY guide raised to +4.5-5.0%, six major wins worth ~200bps. Rejected - see near-misses.
Four days in, the pattern has stopped being a pattern and started being a finding. Ten companies read this run, roughly twenty-five across four days, and the count of non-tech corporates that have voluntarily bridged an AI mention to a revenue number remains zero. The bridge is not missing because AI is absent from these calls - AI is on almost all of them. It is missing because attribution is either not measured or not survivable under disclosure liability. BCG's 22 Jul CEO survey puts a number on that same gap from the inside: only 14% of 152 CEOs at $500m+ companies have clearly defined P&L impact for all their AI initiatives.

The appendix entry, and why it is the day's real signal

Infosys is out of scope as a subject - an IT services firm selling AI implementation is a shovel vendor, and its AI revenue is its own sales, not a corporate's deployment ROI. It goes to the appendix and is never counted. But it is the sharpest disclosure on the tape, for a reason that inverts the usual reading.

The metric is unusually honest by construction. Per Dataquest's deep dive of the call, 8.2% covers six named value pools and excludes conventional engagements with AI tools bolted on - the exclusion that turns most AI-revenue metrics into the denominator games METHOD rejects. On that tighter definition AI revenue went from ~5.5% to 8.2% in two quarters.

And in the same release the company cut its growth guidance. Management named the mechanism - client pressure to deliver existing work with "fewer people, fewer hours or lower costs", reported as "AI-led deflation" - and conceded the two effects "cannot always be separated cleanly" because clients bundle productivity concessions into the same contracts that award new AI work. HCLTech is cited in the same piece estimating portfolio deflation at 2-3%; not logged, second-hand and undated.

Gross AI revenue up ~50% in two quarters; net top line guided down. If the firm whose entire business model is selling AI cannot show AI as net additive to its own top line, the prior on non-tech incumbents showing it should fall, not rise. That is a harder constraint on the S2 story than any of the null surveys already in this log, because it comes from the party with the strongest incentive to report the opposite.

Near-misses deliberately not logged

  • Publicis Groupe - the CEO's claim that 87% of net revenue is "AI-powered marketing services" is a denominator game of exactly the kind METHOD names, and the ~20% three-year growth "4 times faster than our competition" is a comparative on a segment defined circularly as the AI-powered one. Decisive test: the primary press release contains one AI sentence and attributes no growth to AI. When the quantified AI claim exists only in the call narration and not in the printed release, that asymmetry is the tell. Note the three retrieved summaries disagreed on H1 organic growth (4.7% vs 5.3% vs the release's +4.8% Q2 / +4.5% Q1) - a second reason to hold to the release.
  • Ford / BlueCruise and GM / Super Cruise (carried from 28 Jul) - fail the same test (c). ADAS subscriptions are real new-SKU revenue and growing +20%, but the capability shipped in 2017-2021. Neither company calls it AI. Reading it as AI revenue would be attribution laundering performed by the reader, not the issuer.
  • IQVIA - 294 agents, 90 use cases, 19 of 20 top pharma. Adoption counting, not a velocity metric and not revenue: a deployment census tells you nothing about throughput or hit rate. Also arguably out of scope as a subject. Fails the bar on the number, not on scope.
  • BCG CEO survey (22 Jul, n=152, $500m+ revenue) - the 14%-defined-P&L-impact figure is quoted above because it corroborates the disclosure gap, but the headline ("nearly nine in ten CEOs see cost or revenue benefits") is self-reported sentiment, no fielding date is given, and the log already holds two better-instrumented measurements of the same phenomenon. A third would be padding, not convergence.
  • Schneider Electric, Siemens, Nokia - AI-attributed revenue that is datacentre and grid capex sold into the buildout. Shovel revenue. Same rejection as 28 Jul.
  • Agentic-commerce sweep - returned only vendor and aggregator statistics again (conversion rates, referred-traffic shares). No named merchant disclosed a figure. Tier D, third run in a row it has come back empty.

Nearest dated catalysts

  • Thu 30 - Fri 31 Jul: back half of the non-tech Q2 tape. Insurers and consumer names still to print.
  • Thu 13 Aug: Heartflow Q2 2026 (confirmed date). AI-derived FFR-CT with its own reimbursement pathway is the single closest thing to a clean S3 on any calendar - a diagnostic sold to patients whose prior alternative was invasive angiography or nothing. Q1 revenue grew 41% and guidance was raised. Whether it is in scope or appendix turns on how the company describes itself; the counterfactual tests should be run properly on that print.
  • Infosys Q2 FY27 (Oct): does AI share keep rising while cc growth guidance stays at or below 3%? That is this entry's falsifier and the cleanest near-term test of the substitution thesis.
  • CMS CY2027 payment rules: still the clearest structural S3 enabler in view. Standing item until a named provider books revenue against a new AI code.
  • Neptune Q3 (Oct): does a quote-to-bind conversion number ever appear? Carried from 28 Jul.

No findings cleared the bar for the main revenue log. 1 out-of-scope finding in the appendix below. Infosys IT services · India S2 — Share⚠ Counter-evidence

In the Q1 FY27 results release (23 Jul 2026) Infosys disclosed "AI as a % of Revenue" of 8.2% on total revenue of $5,082m (derived: approx. $417m), up from approximately 5.5% two quarters earlier. CEO Salil Parekh: "AI momentum is now rapidly converting into revenue, which demonstrates how Infosys' differentiated enterprise AI value proposition is translating into consistent market share gains." In the same release Infosys narrowed FY27 constant-currency revenue growth guidance to 1.5%-3.0% from 1.5%-3.5%, with reported growth of 2.4% YoY in constant currency.

Mechanism
Infosys sells AI-first services across six named value pools - AI strategy, process redesign, agents, data modernisation, AI engineering, platform-led implementation. Per Dataquest's account of the call, the metric deliberately EXCLUDES conventional engagements that merely have AI tools bolted onto existing workflows; those are tracked internally and not disclosed. That is a materially tighter definition than the 'AI-influenced revenue' constructs METHOD rejects, and it is the reason this is loggable at all. Running the other way, management described client pressure to use coding assistants and agents to complete existing work with fewer people and hours - reported as 'AI-led deflation' - compressing application maintenance, infrastructure operations and software development revenue, and said the two effects 'cannot always be separated cleanly' because clients bundle productivity concessions into contracts that also award new AI work.
Why this shape
S2, and the CEO says so himself: 'market share gains'. Absent Infosys' AI capability these buyers would have bought transformation and engineering services from Accenture, TCS, Wipro, Cognizant or Capgemini - test (a) fails for S3, a competitor lost the sale. Test (c) is arguably passed (agentic delivery is a genuine post-2024 capability) but capability novelty alone does not make new demand. Zero-sum at industry level; GDP-neutral. Subject is out of scope for the main log in METHOD's own terms - an IT services firm selling AI implementation is a shovel vendor, and its AI revenue describes its own sales rather than a corporate's deployment ROI - so it is routed to the appendix and never counted. Flagged counter_evidence because the pairing is the finding: AI revenue share rose roughly 50% in two quarters and the company cut total growth guidance in the same release. Gross AI revenue up, net top line down. This is the sharpest quantification yet found of AI revenue substituting for the base rather than adding to it - and it comes from a firm whose whole business is selling AI, which is the population most incentivised to show the opposite.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Infosys Q1 FY27 results release / Form 6-K, 23 Jul 2026; definition of the metric and the deflation commentary from Dataquest deep dive of the same call (Tier C, secondary), 2026-07-23
Falsifier
If AI-first revenue share keeps climbing through FY27 (Q2 and Q3) while constant-currency total growth guidance stays at or below 3%, the substitution read is confirmed and the 8.2% is a mix metric, not a growth metric. Conversely, if total cc growth guidance is raised above 3% while AI share rises, AI revenue is genuinely additive and this entry is wrong. Second falsifier: if Infosys stops publishing 'AI as a % of Revenue' - a disclosure a company retires when the number stops flattering it.
Confidence
medium — The 8.2%, the revenue base and the guidance are Tier A primary. The six-value-pool definition, the ~5.5% prior-quarter comparator and the 'AI-led deflation' language reach me only through one trade-press deep dive of the call, not the transcript itself - load-bearing and second-hand, so flagged.

2026-07-28

Assessment

Empty day in the main log — zero findings, no S3, not even a candidate. Twenty search angles, six Q2 2026 transcripts read in full. One out-of-scope finding recorded in the appendix. The gap today was not a shortage of AI talk; it was that every AI mention arrived without a number attached to revenue, and every large revenue number arrived without AI attached to it.

Sources read

  • UPS 2Q 2026 release (28 Jul, the only in-window primary print reviewed): revenue $22.8bn, FY guide raised to ~$91.2bn, $1.2bn of H1 transformation benefits — all attributed to network reconfiguration and efficiency, with no AI attribution anywhere in the release. A large logistics incumbent mid-transformation declining to put the AI label on its own savings is worth noting.
  • General Motors Q2 2026: OnStar revenue $800m, +20% YoY; deferred revenue $6.3bn, up nearly 50%; Super Cruise attach rate 30-40%; ~1m new subscriptions expected in 2026. The single largest genuinely-new-SKU revenue line found this run — and GM does not call it AI. Date discrepancy flagged: the call was 21 Jul, the OnStar detail surfaced in trade coverage dated 27 Jul.
  • Verizon Q2 2026 (24 Jul): churn 84bps (-6bps YoY), promo CAC -15%, Simplicity gross adds 16% better than forecast. None of it attributed to AI. The AI-linked revenue is the Google dark-fibre deal — >$1bn, contributing "beginning in 2027." That is selling capacity to the buildout, not ROI from deployment.
  • BNY Q2 2026 (22 Jul): ~40% of engineer-written software now AI-generated; management calls AI "a capacity creator," says impact is "tangible, and measurable" — and then discloses no measure. AI spend inside the $4bn engineering budget described as "de minimis."
  • The Hartford Q2 2026 (24 Jul): underwriting activities "completed in a fraction of the time"; small-business written premium +7%. The 7% is not attributed to the AI and the "fraction" is not quantified. Fails the bar on both halves.
  • Intuitive Surgical Q2 2026 (17 Jul): revenue $2.89bn +19%, recurring 85% of total. Exactly one AI sentence in the entire call, listing it among R&D domains. No standalone disclosure for Case Insights or Intuitive Hub.
  • Neptune Insurance Q2 2026 (release 21 Jul, call 22 Jul): revenue $55.9m +33%, written premium $126.9m +31%, loss ratio 19.5%, FY26 guide $199m. Logged to the appendix — see below.
The pattern, three days in: the disclosure gap is not random — it is structural. Where AI is named, no number follows; where a number is disclosed, AI is not named. Seven calls this run, and not one company voluntarily bridged the two. Firms quantify what they are proud of and what investors demand; AI revenue attribution is so far in neither category. That silence is the finding, and it is repeating.

Appendix entry, and the schema change behind it

Neptune Insurance is the sharpest disclosure found today and it sat outside the schema. It is logged to the appendix under a rule change made this run: scope: appendix now accepts any shape, not S3 only. The original S3-only constraint would have discarded a quantified S1 from a Tier A call — the wrong failure, because an entrant's S1 is the natural comparator for the incumbent silence catalogued above. Neptune is out of scope in its own words (an "AI-native managing general agent" that "operates without human underwriters"), so it is never counted in the totals; it is kept visible so the incumbent-vs-entrant split stays measurable.

The S3 read on Neptune was run and failed on two independent tests, which is worth recording because it is the single most gameable claim in this sector: >55% of new business outside mandatory flood zones and >75% voluntary purchase looks like new demand, but NFIP already writes voluntary policies outside the SFHA — test (b) fails — and Burgess said flatly "We're not using AI to set pricing... All of the AI that we're using is algorithmic" — test (c) fails. A company growing 33% with AI in its self-description, and its own CEO closing off the capability-gate argument.

Near-misses deliberately not logged

  • GM / Super Cruise — fails test (c) hard. Super Cruise shipped in 2017; the +20% OnStar growth is distribution expansion across more equipped models, not a frontier capability jump. Logging it as AI revenue would be attribution laundering of exactly the kind METHOD warns about. Note that GM itself is not making the claim — the temptation here is entirely on the reader's side.
  • Nokia Q2 2026 — "AI sales surge 105%." Reject: telecom-equipment revenue sold into AI datacentre capex. Shovel revenue, not deployment ROI. Same rejection applies to the big-bank fee income from SpaceX-style AI megadeals.
  • Massimo Group — $19.7m / up-to-5,000-unit commitment for a "new AI-enabled product category." Reject on four counts: dated 13 Jan 2026 (out of window), counterparty unnamed, product undisclosed, and the company states revenue recognition "remain[s] subject to various contingencies." A micro-cap LOI is not a result.
  • AIG / Great American / W.R. Berkley AI-liability exclusions — real counter-evidence to the "insurers open new TAM by pricing AI risk" thesis, but the filings are Nov 2025 and the reporting April 2026. Too stale to log as new; carried forward as a standing signal.
  • Agentic-commerce and AI-drive-thru sweeps — returned only vendor and consultancy material. No deploying company disclosed a referred-sales or check-average figure. Tier D, not logged.

Nearest dated catalysts

  • Wed 29 Jul - Fri 31 Jul: peak week of the non-tech Q2 tape. Watch the consumer and industrial prints, not the megacap tech ones.
  • Q2 insurer prints still to come: Progressive, Allstate, Lemonade — the sector where an S3 is most likely to appear if one exists at all.
  • CMS CY2027 payment rules: the 2026 path for paying AI as a medical service is the clearest structural S3 enabler in view. When a named provider or device maker first books revenue against a new AI code, that is a genuine S3 — check each quarter.
  • Neptune Q3 (Oct): does a quote-to-bind conversion number ever appear alongside the Atlas+ engagement counts? That is the appendix entry's falsifier and the cleanest near-term test of whether S1 disclosures convert.
  • GM Q3 (Oct): does Super Cruise attach rate hold at 30-40% as it rolls to lower-priced models? Not an AI finding, but the best available proxy for whether a legacy incumbent can sustain a software SKU.
