Neptune Insurance Holdings Insurance · US S1 — Velocity

On the Q2 2026 call (22 Jul 2026) CEO Trevor Burgess said the newly live quote-to-bind model is "definitely having a positive impact on the new business sales"; ~3,700 agents exchanged 33,000 messages with the Atlas+ assistant during the quarter. Q2 revenue $55.9m (+33% YoY), written premium $126.9m (+31%), lifetime written loss ratio 19.5%, FY26 revenue guided to $199m. None of the revenue is attributed to either system.

Mechanism
Two distinct systems in the revenue path. (1) A machine-learning quote-to-bind model inside Triton, the underwriting platform, scoring and routing submissions with no human underwriter in the loop. (2) Atlas+, an agent-facing assistant sitting on the quote screen answering the writing agent's questions in real time and drafting follow-up email, with a ranked next-best-task feed due in Q3. The stated route to revenue is higher agent quoting throughput and a better quote-to-bind conversion rate.
Why this shape
S1, not S2 or S3. Every disclosed number is an input metric - agent count, message count - and the revenue statement is qualitative. Management gave a precise written-premium figure ($126.9m) and a precise FY guide ($199m) and attributed neither to the AI: this is the S1 conversion silence in its purest form, from a company that is otherwise unusually forthcoming. The S3 read was run and failed: >55% of new business outside mandatory flood zones and >75% voluntary purchase looks like new demand, but NFIP already writes voluntary policies outside the SFHA, so test (b) fails on prior alternative; and Burgess said flatly "We're not using AI to set pricing... All of the AI that we're using is algorithmic," which fails test (c) on the capability gate. Appendix, not main, because the subject is out of scope in its own words - the 21 Jul release calls Neptune an "AI-native managing general agent" that "operates without human underwriters." It does not test whether an ordinary incumbent gets revenue ROI from AI spend; it tests whether an AI-native entrant can take share in a boring line. Logged so that comparator stays visible rather than being discarded.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Q2 2026 earnings call (22 Jul); figures corroborated by the 21 Jul results release, 2026-07-22
Falsifier
By the Q4 2026 / FY26 call, a disclosed quote-to-bind conversion delta or bound-premium dollar figure attributable to the model. If Atlas+ engagement counts keep growing across Q3 and Q4 while the conversion number never appears, the quote-to-bind claim was narrative and the 31-33% growth was distribution and pricing, not AI.
Confidence
medium — Tier A call, named executive, on-the-record quotes, and hard quarter figures from the results release - but zero of the revenue is AI-attributed, and an AI-native subject cannot test the incumbent thesis this log exists to test.

Assessment

Two findings, and the day's real signal is an absence with an unusually clean shape. No S3. No S3-CANDIDATE. Second consecutive day with zero new-TAM evidence from a non-tech incumbent.

Transcripts read directly

The week of 20–24 July 2026 was a heavy non-tech Q2 print week.

  • American Express — AI discussed exclusively in cost and risk terms (Squeri: coding cycle time down 30–40%; fraud/credit AI "for 15, 16 years"). Zero revenue attributed to it.
  • Baker Hughes — names Cordant and Leucipa deployments with SINOPEC, Petrobras and KNPC, and puts no revenue figure on either. The press release contains no instance of the word AI at all.
  • Neptune Insurance — revenue +33% to $55.9m, premium in force +32% to $419m, lifetime written loss ratio 19.5% credited to its Triton engine.   • Honeywell — the exception that proves it. The largest AI-linked top-line number of the quarter from a non-tech industrial: ~$900m → just over $1bn of software ARR at ~15% growth, with the CEO calling Forge "the primary mechanism for delivering physical AI" — and not one dollar of that guided ARR attributed to AI.
The pattern is now visible across two consecutive runs. Non-tech incumbents reaffirm AI framing and AI capex while the revenue-attributed number is either absent (Honeywell, AmEx, Baker Hughes) or has quietly disappeared from the prior disclosure (Albertsons, logged 26 Jul).

The Grant Thornton manufacturing cut quantifies that gap from the other direction: 64% report efficiency gains, 0% report significant revenue uplift — in the sector with the highest deployment rate.

Three near-misses deliberately NOT logged

  • Swiss Re data-centre premium pool ($10.6bn → a projected $24.2bn by 2030). Genuinely new demand, but it is demand created by AI capex, not revenue produced by an AI capability — it fails the capability gate, since underwriting a data centre needs no frontier model. That distinction matters and will recur: insuring the buildout is a shovel, not a result.
  • CMS 2027 OPPS proposed rule creating a "Software as a Medical Service" payment category with 36 HCPCS codes. This is the reimbursement gate opening for genuine healthcare S3, but it names no company and carries no dollar figure — a precondition, not a finding. Watch it.
  • Deere Precision Essentials (~70% renewal, >90% second-year cohort, 4,000+ new customer organizations). The best evidence anywhere of a new-SKU AI subscription retaining customers — but it dates to the 21 May 2026 call and discloses adoption metrics with zero revenue dollars.

Nearest dated catalysts

  • GE HealthCare — Q2, 29 Jul
  • Caterpillar — Q2, 4 Aug
  • HeartFlow — Q2, 13 Aug
Honeywell International Industrials / Industrial automation · US S2 — Share

On the Q2 2026 call (23 Jul 2026) CEO Vimal Kapur guided software ARR growth of ~15% for 2026 and said the business will "inch from about $900 million to a little over $1 billion of ARR in that segment this year." He described Forge as "the primary mechanism for delivering 'physical AI' to customers" and named software ARR as "the best way to measure our progress" toward autonomy. No revenue was attributed to AI itself.

Mechanism
Honeywell Forge is the connected-plant software layer sold on top of Honeywell's installed automation base (Kapur: "we have been selling automation since 1975"). It is sold as subscription ARR, and the AI/autonomy content is being positioned as the reason customers move up the subscription tiers. The revenue mechanism is software attach onto existing hardware relationships, not a new product with no predecessor.
Why this shape
S2. Test: absent this AI capability, would the buyer have bought something similar from somebody? Yes - industrial asset-performance and process-optimization software is a contested category (AVEVA, Siemens, GE Vernova, ABB, Rockwell). Growth here is share taken inside an existing budget line, zero-sum at industry level and GDP-neutral. Explicitly NOT S3: Forge has a predecessor in Honeywell's own catalog going back years, and no capability gate at the 24-month frontier is established. The attribution weakness is the point of logging it: this is the largest AI-linked top-line number a non-tech industrial put on the record this quarter, and management still did not attribute a single dollar of it to AI - only to "Forge offerings" and "the launch of new software products." 'Physical AI' is the framing wrapped around a pre-existing software line.
Status
PROJECTED — forward target, not a result
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Honeywell Q2 2026 earnings call transcript (Motley Fool); 15% ARR figure corroborated in Investing.com transcript, 2026-07-23
Falsifier
Forge/segment software ARR failing to print above $1.0bn on the Q4 2026 / FY26 results, or the ~15% ARR growth rate not being restated on the Q3 2026 call. Second and sharper falsifier: if Honeywell never breaks out an AI-attributed revenue figure across FY26-FY27 while continuing to describe Forge as its physical-AI delivery mechanism, treat the AI framing as a repositioning of existing software revenue rather than an AI result.
Confidence
medium — The 15% ARR growth guide is corroborated across two independent transcripts. The $900m-to-$1bn absolute figure was verified verbatim by direct retrieval but appears in only one transcript source, and Kapur's phrase "that segment" is not unambiguously tied to a named reporting segment in the retrieved text - do not treat the $900m base as an audited segment disclosure. Status is PROJECTED because it is a within-year guide, not a realized print.
Grant Thornton 2026 AI Impact Survey - manufacturing cut (n=100 of 950) Manufacturing / Cross-sector · US S2 — Share⚠ Counter-evidence

Survey of 950 business leaders across 10 industries, fielded 23 Feb - 18 Mar 2026: "Among manufacturing respondents, zero reported significant revenue uplift from AI initiatives, compared to 12% of executives across all industries. Zero reported significant cost savings, again compared to 12% overall." 47% of manufacturers reported only minimal revenue uplift; 64% reported increased efficiency. Manufacturers led all sectors in operational AI deployment (62%) while reporting the weakest financial return.

Mechanism
Not a deployment finding - a base-rate measurement of the population this log samples from, and the cleanest available quantification of the efficiency-without-revenue gap: 64% report efficiency gains, 0% report significant revenue uplift, in the sector with the HIGHEST operational deployment rate. If efficiency mechanically converted to top line, the sector leading deployment should not be the sector reporting zero.
Why this shape
Counter-evidence, tagged to S2 because S2 (attributed incremental revenue on existing products) is the shape most non-tech corporates claim and the shape this null result most directly contradicts. It also constrains S3 by implication: a sector reporting zero significant revenue uplift is not opening new markets. The 58%-vs-15% headline elsewhere in the same survey (fully-integrated vs piloting firms reporting AI-driven revenue growth) is a selection-effect artifact, not causal evidence - firms that integrate deeply are firms that already saw something work - and should not be cited as offsetting the manufacturing zero.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier D — Vendor case study or consultant survey — lowest tier
Grant Thornton, "Manufacturing insights: 2026 AI Impact Survey Report" (survey fielded 23 Feb - 18 Mar 2026; press coverage 9 Jul 2026), 2026-07-09
Falsifier
The 2027 wave of the same survey showing manufacturing significant-revenue-uplift materially above zero, or a Q3/Q4 2026 cluster of named manufacturers disclosing AI-attributed revenue. Either would mean this reading was a timing artifact of a sector mid-J-curve rather than a structural absence.
Confidence
medium — Tier D, and METHOD says Tier D is never the sole basis for an entry. Logged anyway with that conflict stated, on one specific ground: the systematic-inflation bias that justifies distrusting Tier D runs the OTHER WAY here - Grant Thornton sells AI advisory and is publishing a zero against its own commercial interest. Remaining limits are real: self-reported, non-random sample, "significant" undefined by the respondent, n=100 subgroup, and the field window (Feb-Mar 2026) is ~5 months stale relative to today. Age also exceeds the 14-day window; included because it is unlogged and directly load-bearing on the thesis.

2026-07-26

permalink →

Assessment

One finding, and it is a negative one. Off-week for the thesis: no S3, no S3-CANDIDATE, and nothing clearing the bar on the positive side.

The week's non-tech Q2 prints

Strikingly consistent — AI is discussed everywhere, quantified nowhere on the revenue line.

  • Chubb — Q2 call contains no AI mention at all.
  • Travelers — AI described qualitatively (straight-through claims processing, submission data extraction) with zero quantified linkage to premium, retention or hit rate. Volunteers that it is watching token costs.
  • Bank of America — Moynihan gave 400,000 daily prompts, 300+ approved use cases, >20% developer productivity. All input and cost metrics. Consumer revenue growth reported alongside but never attributed.
  • Verizon — separates AI-for-operations (cost) from AI Connect (revenue) and puts the revenue in 2027. Its $1bn Google dark-fiber deal is demand from the AI buildout, not ROI on Verizon's own AI spend — out of frame for this log.
  • SLB — Digital division $697m, +18% y/y; ARR $1.04bn, +15%. But management bundles AI inside Delfi/Lumi/Agora/Tela and isolates no AI-specific figure, so the growth number cannot be attributed.
  • Halliburton — names Zeus iQ, LOGIX and the Sekal acquisition as automation differentiators, no dollar attached.
  • Delta — mentions "AI-powered personalization" once, unquantified. Its Fetcherr pricing work is under political scrutiny and Delta's own on-record statement declines to give any percentage of fares or revenue effect.

Rejected on method rather than substance

  • RELX (H1 2026, 23 Jul) — disclosed that ~90% of new sales value and ~three-quarters of renewal value in Legal now runs through the AI-enabled Lexis+/Protégé platform, against 10% underlying Legal growth. That is the denominator game METHOD names explicitly: AI-touched share of bookings is not incremental revenue. RELX's subject status is borderline anyway.
  • PwC 2026 AI performance study and FMI grocery technology survey — consultant/trade sentiment, no named company.
  • AWS 3.5x shopping-assistant conversion claim — Tier D vendor, tech subject.
  • Munich Re aiSure and the Beazley–AIUC AI liability paper — genuinely new insurance classes, but they are demand created by other people's AI risk, not revenue from the insurer's own AI deployment, and no premium figure is disclosed.

The signal: Albertsons

In January the CEO put a specific, checkable revenue-side number on the record — Ask AI delivering a 10% basket-size lift.

Six months later, on 23 July, the same CEO reaffirmed AI as foundational, laid out four enterprise AI priorities (three of them cost-side), guided $1.9–2.0bn of capex toward stores and AI — and gave no AI-attributed revenue metric at all. Digital sales grew 13%, unattributed.

Companies repeat numbers that are working. The disclosure asymmetry — specific quantified cost and productivity metrics from BofA, specific quantified capex from Albertsons, and qualitative adjectives everywhere the revenue line should be — is the actual pattern across every non-tech Q2 print read today, and it is what this log exists to measure.

Second pass (manual re-run, same day, varied phrasing)

No new positive findings; one further counter-evidence entry added.

  • Honeywell — Q2 call contains no substantive AI discussion at all. Its datacenter exposure is sold as fire, security, on-site power and liquid cooling: demand from the AI buildout, not AI deployed to produce revenue.
  • Roche — H1 gives two AI mentions, both forward-looking: the PathAI acquisition in digital pathology, and a promise to address AI in early drug development at Pharma Day on 28 Sep 2026. No revenue, no timeline, no addressable-population number.
  • Mechanism-term sweep (quote-to-bind, attach rate, next-best-action, retail media, hotel yield management) returned vendor marketing exclusively, with no carrier, retailer or operator putting its own number on the record.

The confound worth naming

The added MIT FutureTech / CMU 10-K adoption study reframes the empty positive column. Two-thirds of deeply integrated enterprise AI adoption sits in the technology sector; non-tech adoption is described as only slowly accelerating. This log excludes tech as subjects by design, so it is sampling the thin end of the distribution.

That is a real confound: an empty log is consistent both with "AI does not yet produce measurable top-line revenue at incumbents" and with "incumbents have not yet deeply adopted it" — and nothing found today distinguishes the two. The falsifier on that entry is written to force the distinction over the next two 10-K vintages.

Noted for method: the entry breaches inclusion-bar rule 1 (named company), because no aggregate study can satisfy it, while the counter-evidence clause explicitly names null studies as loggable. Those two rules conflict; this run resolved in favour of the counter-evidence clause.

Albertsons Companies Grocery / Retail · US S2 — Share⚠ Counter-evidence

On its Q1 FY2026 call (23 Jul 2026) Albertsons reaffirmed AI as "foundational" and guided FY26 capex of $1.9-2.0bn toward store modernization and AI capabilities, but disclosed no AI-attributed revenue metric. The 10% Ask AI basket-size lift CEO Susan Morris put on the record on the 7 Jan 2026 call was not repeated. Digital sales grew 13%, not attributed to AI.

Mechanism
Ask AI conversational search in the Albertsons/Safeway/Vons/Jewel-Osco mobile apps surfaces complementary items mid-search, expanding the basket; household-level personalized offers on top of ~tens of millions of loyalty households. The mechanism is real and the January number was specific; what is missing is any repeat measurement.
Why this shape
S2, and counter-evidence to it. Basket expansion inside an existing customer base buying existing groceries in existing stores - absent the AI, these shoppers buy groceries from somebody. Zero-sum at industry level, GDP-neutral. Logged as counter-evidence because the only quantified revenue-side AI metric this company had ever disclosed vanished from the next disclosure while AI capex was reaffirmed and expanded. Four named enterprise AI priorities were given (digital CX, merchandising intelligence, labor optimization, supply chain optimization); three of the four are cost-side, and the revenue-side one carried no number.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier A — Primary and accountable — filing, transcript, formal guidance
Q1 FY2026 earnings call, complete transcript (prior claim: Q3 FY2025 call, 7 Jan 2026, via PYMNTS / CX Today), 2026-07-23
Falsifier
Reverse-falsifier: if Albertsons re-discloses the Ask AI basket-size lift, or any successor revenue-attributed AI metric at or above 10%, on the Q2 FY2026 call (expected Oct 2026), this entry is a reporting artifact and should be downgraded. If two more quarters pass with AI capex reaffirmed and still no AI-attributed revenue number, treat the January figure as a one-off marketing disclosure rather than a managed KPI.
Confidence
medium — Absence-of-evidence is weaker than a stated walk-back; the July transcript was read via a summarizing fetch, so a passing mention could have been missed. The January 10% figure is Tier C reporting (two independent outlets) of a Tier A call, not the transcript itself.
S&P 500 (510 firms, 2016-2025) - MIT FutureTech / CMU study Cross-sector · US S2 — Share⚠ Counter-evidence

Peer-style study of AI adoption in 510 S&P 500 firms, scored from SEC 10-K language: in 2025 only 11% had AI "deeply integrated into their business processes", plus a further 10% "using AI in the production of goods and delivery of services", up from 5% in 2022. The technology sector "accounts for two-thirds of deeply integrated enterprise adoption", with only "slowly accelerating adoption among non-technology firms". Profitability follows a J-curve from no adoption to deep adoption, but the authors "observe no differences in capex or productivity".

Mechanism
Not a deployment finding - a measurement of the population this log samples from. Adoption is scored off 10-K language, which sits under liability for materially false statements, so it is a harder measure than survey self-report. The finding that matters here is the denominator: two-thirds of genuinely deep enterprise AI adoption sits inside the technology sector, i.e. inside the population METHOD excludes as subjects.
Why this shape
Counter-evidence, tagged to S2 because S2 is where the bulk of revenue-attribution claims live, but it cuts against S3 equally. A J-curve in profitability with no measured productivity or capex difference is a margin story, not a top-line story; the paper finds no top-line effect to report. Directly bears on why this log's positive column is empty: the non-tech incumbents it tracks are precisely the slow-adopting tail, so scarce revenue disclosure may reflect thin real adoption rather than adoption without payoff. Those are very different worlds and this study does not separate them.
Status
DISCLOSED — realized and reported
Source
Tier C — Third-party reporting — journalism, trade press, sell-side
Yu, Fleming, Hampton, Combemale & Thompson, "AI Adoption in S&P 500 Firms", arXiv 2607.08920 (submitted 9 Jul 2026), 2026-07-09
Falsifier
If a later vintage of this measure (2026-27 10-Ks) shows non-tech deep adoption converging toward tech-sector levels while revenue-side disclosure stays absent, the 'thin adoption' explanation dies and 'adoption without top-line payoff' is the live one. If non-tech deep adoption stays under ~10% of firms, this log should expect a structurally empty positive column and should not read that as evidence AI does not produce revenue.
Confidence
medium — Primary abstract retrieved and quoted directly; paper body not read this run. Preprint, not yet peer reviewed. Secondary coverage (theaiinsider.tech, 14 Jul) additionally claims the study found 'little evidence that AI adoption was associated with higher revenue per employee' - that line is NOT in the abstract and is unverified against the paper body, so it is excluded from the claim above. Source is 17 days old, outside the trailing-48h window, logged as a strong prior-fortnight item.
